Nominal Neutral Rate
global balance of saving and investment will have a large influence on the neutral rate in Australia, though they

are not the only influence (see below). Global forces relevant to the neutral rate include productivity growth,
demographic trends and government debt levels.
Estimates of neutral rates have increased in the post-pandemic period
and there are a range of possible explanations.
Estimates of the neutral rate in Australia have increased over recent years to be above their
pre-pandemic levels, partly reversing a long-run decline (Graph B.1). A similar trend is evident in many

advanced economies, including the United States.3 Although the increase over recent years is relevant to the
assessment of the current stance of monetary policy, understanding it requires considering why neutral rate
estimates declined over the decades leading up to the pandemic. The research literature has put forward two
competing explanations. First, the decline could reflect slow-moving structural factors, such as demographics,
weak productivity growth and inequality, that caused ‘secular stagnation’. Second, the particularly sharp decline

in neutral rate estimates in the 2010s could be picking up more temporary factors, such as the need to repair
balance sheets in the aftermath of the global financial crisis (GFC).4
The first explanation, secular stagnation, argues
that households and businesses increasingly
wished to save more, relative to firms’ desired
investment, placing downward pressure on
interest rates.5 Three possible causes identified in
the literature are demographic change, weak

productivity growth and rising inequality globally.
Population ageing and rising inequality can increase
desired saving, while weaker productivity growth can
reduce desired investment. Together, these factors
will put downward pressure on interest rates. At the
same time, a number of governments globally ran
substantial fiscal deficits, including because of
increased social security expenditure to support
ageing populations. The literature argues that the

decline in neutral rates would probably have been
even larger without these fiscal deficits.
A second explanation is that the unsustainable credit boom in the 2000s and the balance sheet
damage caused by the resulting GFC placed persistent, but ultimately temporary, downward pressure
on interest rates.6 This literature argues that many of the slow-moving structural factors associated with
secular stagnation do not align with either the steep decline in estimated neutral rates during the 2010s or

their subsequent increase. For example, the working-age population has declined relative to the old-age
population in the OECD in a relatively steady way that does not match the sharper decline and then rebound in
neutral rate estimates. Instead, this literature argues that high levels of household and business debt and large
falls in asset prices led households, businesses and financial intermediaries to spend a prolonged period
repairing their balance sheets. This desire to increase private saving and reduce private investment worked to

lower neutral rates. As the effects of the GFC faded and balance sheets were repaired, this downward influence
on neutral should have dissipated.
While the estimates presented above suggest that neutral interest rates have increased materially
since the pandemic, the drivers of that increase remain uncertain. The increase over recent years is
broadly consistent with explanations that emphasise the fading effects of the GFC, but it could also reflect

Neutral Rate – Fundamental Drivers
changes in longer-running structural factors (Graph B.2; Table B.1). For example, the post-pandemic rise in
neutral rate estimates has coincided with a rapid expansion in AI-related investment and stronger US
productivity growth than in the 2010s. Higher expected productivity can raise the expected return on

investment and encourage businesses to invest more, placing upward pressure on neutral rates. Large and
persistent fiscal deficits in advanced overseas economies could also be putting upward pressure on neutral rate
estimates by reducing public saving. As a share of GDP, post-pandemic deficits in the United States and
elsewhere have been larger than in the late 2010s or before the GFC.
The magnitude of these effects remains highly
uncertain.7 Structural changes, such as in expected

future productivity, are hard to measure and do not
necessarily occur gradually nor independently of
other forces. Newer and ongoing structural changes
are likely to be evolving in their effect on neutral
rates. The effects of climate change, for example, are
ambiguous: additional investment in renewables,
including to meet the energy requirements of AI,
could put upward pressure on neutral rates, whereas
the higher returns required by investors to

compensate for physical climate risks could work in
the opposite direction.8 Globally, government deficits
have also continued to increase, and political and
geopolitical factors could sustain this trend. At the
same time, ageing populations and rising inequality
continue to weigh on neutral rates.

### Page 34

The global neutral rate is not the only driver of the neutral rate in
Australia.
As mentioned above, global forces have a strong influence on the Australian neutral rate because of
Australia’s role as a small open economy. However, they do not determine it completely. One study
suggests that changes in US neutral rates explain around half of the variation in Australian neutral rate
estimates.9 In theory, once the economy is in balance, shocks have dissipated and monetary policy is therefore

no longer responding to domestic or international shocks, the neutral rate in a small open economy should
equal the global neutral rate plus a risk premium. This risk premium could reflect impediments to the free flow
of capital or differences in the compensation that domestic and international investors require for risk.
There remain considerable uncertainties about both the level and drivers
of the neutral rate, which are consequential for policy.

Although neutral interest rates are an important conceptual framework for considering the stance of
policy, they cannot be observed directly and are difficult to measure. Different approaches to measuring
neutral rates offer different perspectives. Some approaches infer neutral from financial market prices and some
are estimated by examining the evolution of macroeconomic variables. The RBA accounts for this uncertainty
by examining a range of models. Another source of uncertainty arises from how these statistical models are

estimated, meaning that even a single model can produce a wide range of plausible estimates of the neutral
rate.10 And, as noted above, it is difficult to tell in real time how much each model reflects long-run trends
rather than shorter-term developments.
Reflecting this uncertainty, policymakers are cautious when interpreting (small) short-term moves.
Most models suggest that neutral rates have increased over recent years. However, the rise in long-term bond

yields over the year to date could reflect a number of factors other than changes in neutral rates, such as
expectations about future inflation and the additional compensation that investors require to hold longer-term
bonds (see Box A: How Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect Financial Conditions). An increase in
long-term yields therefore does not, by itself, mean that the neutral rate has risen.
Even if neutral rates have increased, it is difficult to determine what has driven the increase. The

relevance of the long-run neutral rate estimates to current policy is mainly the information they contain about
underlying developments in the economy, and whether those are adequately reflected in the RBA’s forecasts.
The persistence and effects of these developments matter for the appropriate setting of policy over the next
few years, as captured by the short-run concept of neutral.
As an example, suppose the emergence of AI technologies leads to a modest increase in global productivity in

the long term. This could place some upward pressure on long-run neutral rate estimates without necessarily
significantly affecting near-term inflation or the appropriate cash rate today. However, if AI also increased
near-term aggregate demand by increasing investment in data centres and related infrastructure, then the
appropriate policy response could be to increase the cash rate by more than the change in the long-run
neutral rate. Given the uncertainty around both the extent of any increase in neutral rates and the forces

driving it, the possibility of a higher neutral rate is partially embedded in the current forecasts and is otherwise
being monitored as a source of uncertainty for the outlook (see Chapter 3: Outlook).

The RBA will continue to develop our understanding of the structural drivers of neutral rates and the degree to
which they are likely to have implications over different horizons for the appropriate setting of monetary policy.
Key areas for research include connecting movements in the neutral rate suite to fundamental economic
drivers, including better understanding the ‘wedge’ between domestic and global neutral rates.

Summary
Global cost pressures from the Middle East conflict and AI boom have increased since
August.
In Australia, GDP growth has slowed this year to a little below its potential growth rate.
Chapter 2
Economic Conditions
• The re-escalation of the Middle East conflict has pushed up energy prices, as global
energy supply continues to be constrained by disruptions to shipping and damage to
refineries. Crude oil and refined product prices have risen by 20–25 per cent and are

materially higher than was assumed in the August forecasts. Prices of a range of base
metals and agricultural commodities have also increased this year, including because of
the conflicts in the Middle East and Ukraine and adverse weather.
• Global economic activity has been more resilient than expected, underpinned by
the AI boom and supportive fiscal measures. GDP growth in Australia’s major trading
partners has trended higher over the past few years. Recently, the strength in global

AI-related demand and fiscal measures in some major economies have more than offset
the negative impacts of the Middle East conflict. The underlying strength in the global
economy has added to global cost and price pressures, with the surge in data centre
activity creating capacity constraints in related supply chains.
• The extent to which global cost pressures will pass through to consumer prices is
uncertain. Headline inflation has increased in most economies following increases in

energy prices, while core inflation is already above central bank targets in many advanced
economies. Policy rate expectations implied by market pricing have increased in many
advanced economies, in line with higher near-term inflationary pressures (see Chapter 1:
Financial Conditions).
• This largely reflects softer growth in household consumption, consistent with the
earlier easing in household income growth, as well as in exports and public demand.

The Middle East conflict is assessed to have had only modest effects on overall domestic
activity so far. Notwithstanding the slowing in growth, recent data suggest that activity
growth this year has been a bit stronger than anticipated in the August forecasts,
supported by very strong investment in data centres.
• Conditions in the housing market have eased and the unemployment rate has
increased, both by more than expected in August. Housing prices have declined

further from their peak in March, primarily reflecting the effects of monetary policy and tax
changes announced in the federal budget. The unemployment rate increased to
4.6 per cent in the September quarter. That appears largely to reflect
stronger-than-expected labour supply; employment growth held up in the September
quarter as expected and liaison and leading indicators suggest a stable near-term outlook
for labour demand.
Reserve Bank of Australia  |  Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 33

### Page 37

Domestic inflation is elevated and has been stronger than expected recently. We now
assess that capacity constraints in the first half of the year were a little tighter than
previously judged.
• Headline inflation was 3.9 per cent over the year to the September quarter and
trimmed mean inflation ticked up to 3.7 per cent. The upside surprise in trimmed mean
inflation – relative to the August forecasts – is judged to reflect that domestic capacity

pressures this year have been stronger than previously assessed, with the indirect effects of
cost pressures related to the Middle East conflict passing through to inflation broadly as
anticipated. While the price of fuel has increased strongly, inflation has been broad-based,
including significant price increases for a range of market services. This is consistent with
elevated growth in unit labour costs. Liaison contacts across most industries continue to

report sustained cost pressures.
• Despite the easing in GDP growth and increase in the unemployment rate we
continue to assess that there are some capacity pressures in the economy. While
below-trend GDP growth and rising unemployment by themselves imply that aggregate
demand and potential supply are moving closer to balance, a range of data – including
persistently high inflation and elevated growth in labour and non-labour input costs –
suggest that domestic capacity constraints are greater than previously assessed and so

capacity pressures remain. Accordingly, we have raised our estimate of the unemployment
rate consistent with low and stable inflation a little, though this estimate remains
very uncertain.
• Some measures of short-term and longer-term inflation expectations have
increased since August. Elevated inflation expectations, including shorter-term
expectations, may contribute to higher realised inflation if they influence wage- and
price-setting decisions today.

Some of the upside risks to global cost pressures
identified in the August Statement have materialised.
Global oil and refined product prices have increased
sharply due to ongoing disruptions related to the Middle
East conflict, and these prices are now expected to
remain elevated for longer. More broadly, a range of
supply disruptions caused by conflicts and adverse
weather events, alongside strong AI-related demand, are
boosting prices of a range of commodities and inflation

in many economies.
Energy prices have increased since
the August Statement.
Front-month Brent crude oil futures prices have
increased by around 25 per cent since the August
Statement and are currently around US$115 per
barrel (Graph 2.1). A renewal of hostilities in the Middle
East led to a sharp increase in global crude oil prices in
September and October, to around their peak level from
earlier this year. That is despite a recovery in crude oil

exports from the Middle East owing to the rerouting of
oil flows and use of ship-to-ship transfers to enable
crude oil flows through the Strait of Hormuz. Elevated
prices likely also reflect an increased geopolitical risk
premium associated with the risk of further disruption to
crude oil flows due to the conflict.

substantially since the August Statement. The spreads
between the prices of refined products and crude oil
(‘crack spreads’) have been elevated for some time,
reflecting supply constraints. Refined product exports
from the Middle East have recovered by less than crude
oil exports in recent months. In addition, global refining

capacity remains constrained due to outages in Russia
and the Middle East, further limiting supplies, particularly
of diesel. Since August, gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices
have increased by around 20–25 per cent. That largely
reflects increases in the cost of buying and shipping oil,
as the rerouting of crude oil and the use of ship-to-ship
transfers have caused congestion and raised shipping
costs for refiners (Graph 2.2). Market reporting indicates

that higher shipping costs have not been fully passed
through to refined product prices, as profit margins for
some Asian refiners have been squeezed. Compressed
margins may discourage refiner production in the near
term, helping maintain elevated refined product prices
and crack spreads.

2.1 Commodity price developments

### Page 39

Low inventory levels and a recovery in Chinese oil
imports leave energy markets vulnerable to further
supply disruptions and upward pressure on prices. A
drawdown in global inventories and a reduction in
Chinese demand for oil imports helped energy markets
absorb the initial impact of the Middle East conflict.
While increases in crude oil supply over August and early
September allowed for a modest restocking of global
crude oil inventories, market analysts estimate that

inventories remain around historically low levels. Refined
product inventories have continued to decline and are
approaching historical minimum levels. Chinese oil
imports have increased recently and a sustained
recovery – for example, to support inventory restocking
– presents an upside risk to global crude oil prices.
Retail petrol and diesel prices in Australia have
increased substantially and are well above the
August forecast assumption and their pre-conflict

levels (Graph 2.3). The increase reflects both the rise in
regional benchmark prices for refined fuel and the
ending of the temporary fuel excise reduction in July
and August. Nonetheless, refined fuel prices, especially
for diesel, remain below the peaks observed earlier this
year, which reflected severe dislocations in Asian refined
product markets following the announcement of export
bans in several exporting countries in the region.

Prices of natural gas have increased in line with
other energy commodities. Asian liquefied natural gas
(LNG) and European natural gas prices have increased by

more than 50 per cent to be around the high levels
observed during the early stages of the Middle East
conflict, driven by restocking demand in Europe ahead
of winter and concerns over future supply from the
Middle East.

### Page 40

Supply disruptions continue to affect
a range of other commodity prices.
Prices of some base metals have risen since August,
while agricultural commodity prices remain
elevated. Copper, zinc and aluminium prices remain
elevated following mining and shipping disruptions,
including due to the Middle East conflict, alongside
continued demand pressure from the energy transition
and AI-related investment. Prices for agricultural
commodities, including wheat, have risen through 2026,

following supply chain disruptions in the Middle East
and Ukraine (Graph 2.4). The increase in fertiliser prices in
March and April as a result of the Middle East conflict
may now also be contributing to elevated agricultural
commodity prices. Adverse weather conditions,
including drought in parts of the United States and
repeated heatwaves in Europe, have also affected
harvests, pushing up grain prices earlier this year.
The internationally declared El Niño event is yet to

materially affect commodity prices but poses risks to
agricultural yields and prices over the year ahead.
The estimated effects on agricultural output and prices
in previous El Niño episodes have varied and tend to
differ across both regions and commodities. El Niño can
increase the likelihood of extreme weather events, which
may exacerbate supply disruptions and contribute to
periods of elevated commodity price volatility.

In terms of key Australian commodity exports, the
price of iron ore has fallen slightly, while the price of
coking coal has increased by 6 per cent since the

August Statement. Iron ore price pressures remain
subdued as supply remains abundant, while coking coal
prices have been boosted by supply disruptions in China
and Indonesia.

Economic growth in Australia’s major trading partners
(MTPs) has trended higher and been consistently
stronger than expected over the past year or so. MTP
growth has been resilient and slightly higher than some
estimates of trend growth recently. Growth has been
supported by the global surge in AI-related investment,
fiscal policy measures targeted at households and
business in many economies, and higher defence
spending in some economies. Against a backdrop of

higher public and private investment, government bond
yields have increased across advanced economies; this
reflects expectations that policy rates will remain higher
for longer, as well as increases in term premia (see
Chapter 1: Financial Conditions). Upstream cost
pressures are intensifying as capacity in AI-related supply
chains struggles to keep pace with soaring demand –
particularly in high-income east Asia – and global energy
prices have been persistently elevated. These pressures

are passing through to some consumer goods prices.
The rise in goods prices has occurred alongside a stalling
in disinflation in services prices, leaving core measures of
inflation elevated relative to central banks’ targets.
Central bank policy responses are discussed in
Chapter 1: Financial Conditions.
MTP growth has been stronger than
expected in the August Statement,
supported by the boom in AI-related
investment that has more than offset
headwinds from the Middle East

conflict.
Growth in Australia’s trading partners remained
resilient in the June and likely also the September
quarters, partly reflecting the global investment
boom, particularly for AI-enabling products. GDP
growth was stronger than expected in economies with
significant exposure to AI-related supply chains, such as
South Korea and Singapore, reflecting robust net exports
and an increase in construction activity. Timely trade
data across several east Asian economies indicate that

export prices continued to rise sharply in the September
quarter – by around 40 per cent in year-ended terms in
South Korea – consistent with manufacturing capacity
being fully utilised, compounding cost pressures from
higher energy prices (Graph 2.5). These upstream cost
pressures have flowed through east Asian supply chains
and into some consumer goods prices.

Outside of east Asia, growth in advanced economies
appears to have remained steady in the September
quarter following stronger-than-expected growth in
the June quarter. Timely indicators point to continued
strength in manufacturing activity and investment in the

September quarter. In the United States, household
consumption and private sector labour demand remain
resilient despite higher energy prices; retail sales growth
over August and September was strong, while
employment growth has remained broadly in line with
estimates of labour supply growth, leaving the

unemployment rate little changed.
Growth in many trading partners has also been
supported by fiscal policies shielding households
and businesses from higher energy costs, as well as
rising defence spending in some economies. In the
first half of the year, many governments implemented
tax cuts, subsidies and fiscal transfers to support
households and businesses through the Middle East
energy shock. While there had been a gradual shift away
from subsidies and price caps for retail fuel, allowing

GDP growth in China rebounded in
the September quarter which,
alongside the announcement of

additional policy support, means
that growth is on track to achieve the
annual GDP growth target.
Chinese economic activity strengthened over the
September quarter, following a period of subdued
activity. Growth was supported by a moderate increase
in consumption and a large positive contribution from
net exports, while investment declined (Graph 2.7).
Household income growth continued to outpace
consumption, lifting the savings ratio amid ongoing

weakness in household sentiment and spending.
Monthly indicators suggest that there was some
improvement in momentum towards the end of the
quarter, led by fixed asset investment and industrial
production as authorities continue to prioritise
development of advanced manufacturing and ‘Six
Networks’ infrastructure projects.1 Local government
special bond issuance increased in August and
September, which should support infrastructure
investment further over the coming months. Property

sector activity continued to decline. Developments in
the Middle East conflict and the AI-investment boom
have affected Chinese trade flows and contributed to
large increases in Chinese export prices.

In response to subdued growth in the middle of the
year, authorities shifted to a more supportive policy
stance and reaffirmed their commitment to achieve
the 2026 annual growth target. The new policy
measures include expanding policy bank lending to
cover infrastructure projects that are part of the ‘Six
Networks’ construction initiative and allowing local

governments to draw on unused special bond quotas
from previous years. Alongside other adjustments to
monetary policy settings and mortgage policies, the
new measures signal that lifting economic growth to
achieve the annual target has become more of a policy
priority, which should support a strong increase in
infrastructure investment over the remainder of
2026 and early 2027.

### Page 43

Core inflation remains elevated in
many economies and energy and
upstream AI cost pressures have
intensified.
Headline inflation has risen across most economies,
driven by higher energy prices following the
re-escalation of hostilities in the Middle East
(Graph 2.8). Most advanced economy central banks have
revised their near-term headline inflation forecasts
higher to reflect elevated energy prices, though most
still expect inflation to return to target by 2028. Available

measures of households’ longer-term inflation
expectations have risen in some advanced economies, in
line with higher energy price inflation. Producer prices
and a range of indicators from timely business surveys
suggest that cost pressures for firms in all advanced
economies remain elevated due to higher energy,
materials and transport prices related to suply
disruptions caused by the Middle East conflict. Some
firms also report limited ability to pass cost increases on

to price-sensitive consumers.
Upstream costs in AI-related supply chains are
passing through to some core consumer goods
prices, while disinflation in services prices has
stalled, leaving core inflation elevated. Core goods
inflation has picked up in many advanced economies
this year and remains above its pre-pandemic averages
in most economies. Prices of some consumer electronics
have risen sharply as upstream costs in AI-related supply
chains have been passed through to related consumer

goods like personal computers. Impacts on aggregate
headline and core measures of inflation have so far been
limited because the affected goods tend to have small
weights in consumer price baskets. Core services
inflation remains above rates consistent with central
bank targets and progress on disinflation in a number of
advanced economies has stalled alongside upward
pressure on transportation costs and signs of
strengthening activity in their services sectors.

GDP growth has slowed to below its potential growth
rate in 2026, as some of the factors that supported
above-trend growth in late 2025 (including strong
growth in income) have waned. The initial phase of the
Middle East conflict looks to have weighed only
modestly on activity growth, as expected. However, the
slowing has been a little more gradual than was
expected in August, largely reflecting
stronger-than-expected business investment related to

data centres and the renewable energy transition.
Household consumption growth has moderated broadly
as expected, despite consumer sentiment being very
low, supported by resilient household balance sheets
and modest real income growth. By contrast, housing
market conditions have eased by more than expected
over recent months.
Growth in domestic activity has
slowed in 2026 following strong
growth over the second half of 2025.
GDP grew by 0.4 per cent in the June quarter, below

its potential growth rate but a little stronger than
expected in the August forecasts (Graph 2.9). Growth
eased in the first half of 2026. That reflected softer
growth in household consumption, exports and public
demand, which outweighed strong growth in dwelling
investment and business investment. The strength in
business investment was relatively broad-based, though
spending related to data centres and renewable energy
projects was particularly strong. Investment in data

centres has mostly been met by stronger imports, which
has limited the impact on overall GDP. Given the usual
lags, the recent tightening in monetary policy and
easing in conditions in the established housing market
are assessed to have had only modest effects on GDP
growth in the first half of the year.

Household consumption growth has
slowed this year to be a little below
trend, consistent with the earlier
slowing in the growth of household
income.
Consumption growth in the June quarter was as
expected, with the net effects from the Middle East
conflict appearing to be modest. Household
consumption grew by 0.4 per cent in the quarter and

year-ended growth slowed to 1.8 per cent alongside
slowing growth in income. Australians’ spending on
overseas travel fell sharply in the quarter, and
consumption of fuel and transport services declined.
However, this was largely offset by stronger electric
vehicle purchases and discretionary
spending domestically.

We assess that consumption growth in the
September quarter was little changed from the June
quarter’s below-trend rate, though given the mixed
signals from the available timely indicators, this
assessment is quite uncertain (Graph 2.10). The ABS
Household Spending Indicator, which captures spending
within Australia, was unchanged in August but points to
strong quarterly growth in nominal spending. However,
other indicators, including liaison and transaction data

from some banks, suggest more modest growth. Vehicle
purchases remained at very high levels in July and
August but are not expected to contribute much to
growth in the quarter. There appears to have been only a
modest recovery in overseas travel so far, based on
spending and departures data.

The moderation in consumption growth this year,
compared with the second half of last year, is
consistent with the earlier slowing in growth in
household income. Year-ended growth in real
household disposable income has slowed materially as
the earlier boost from the Stage 3 tax cuts has waned,
alongside slower growth in labour and rental income

and higher inflation (Graph 2.11). Despite consumer
sentiment remaining near all-time lows (across all
income levels and for both renters and homeowners,
regardless of whether they have a mortgage), the
household saving ratio has been relatively stable since
the start of the year, suggesting little change in
aggregate consumption or saving behaviour so far. This
is consistent with the fact that household balance sheets
remain quite resilient on average – real household net

wealth remains around 37 per cent higher than its
pre-pandemic level – and with the results of previous
analysis that sentiment is typically not an independent
driver of consumption over and above fundamentals
such as income and wealth. However, there is a risk that
the multiple headwinds affecting households and
historically low sentiment have a greater bearing on
household spending decisions than would normally be
expected (see Chapter 3: Outlook).

Business investment grew strongly
over the past year; while increased
investment in data centres and
renewable energy projects were key
drivers, investment picked up across
other industries too.
Business investment in the June quarter was
significantly stronger than expected, increasing by
10.5 per cent over the year (Graph 2.12). While
investment declined a little in the quarter, we had
expected a larger decline following very strong growth
in the March quarter related to data centres.

Non-residential construction, particularly of lumpy
renewable energy projects (unrelated to data centres),
made the largest contribution to the upside surprise on
investment. Data centre investment grew strongly over
the past year but was volatile. Much of this spending
was on imported equipment, which limited the boost to
GDP. Strength in investment was also evident across a
broad range of industries, consistent with the earlier
easing in financial conditions and the upswing in

domestic activity through 2025.

Outside of data centres and renewables, capital
expenditure intentions suggest investment will
decline a little but remain around the average level
of recent years. The effects of tighter monetary policy
and cost pressures related to the Middle East conflict
appear to be dampening capital expenditure intentions
a little. Surveyed business conditions in the September
quarter declined to their lowest level since 2020,
consistent with continued input cost growth in recent

months weighing on profitability, while business
confidence also remains very low. However, there is only
a moderate correlation between business conditions,
business confidence and investment activity.
Notwithstanding the modest decline expected over the
year ahead, firms in the liaison program expect
investment to remain a little above the
long-term average.

Established housing market
conditions have continued to
weaken by more than expected,
driven by a range of factors,
including higher interest rates and
tax changes for investors.
In September, national housing prices were 4.7 per
cent below their recent peak (Graph 2.14). The current
downturn is one of the steepest over the past 50 years,
and price declines have broadened to most capital cities.
Housing market activity has also continued to soften,

with auction clearance rates remaining very low, time on
market rising sharply to above long-run average levels
and housing market turnover declining to low levels.

Dwelling investment was much stronger than
expected over the first half of 2026, although
leading indicators of activity have started to soften,
consistent with the ongoing housing market
downturn (Graph 2.15). Activity was stronger than
expected in the June quarter and there were also
upward revisions to earlier periods, suggesting there is
more capacity in the sector than previously assessed.

Growth in higher density construction and renovation
activity were particularly strong, but construction of
detached housing also increased over the year. Elevated
cost growth and trade availability measures remain
consistent with ongoing capacity pressures. Consistent
with the usual lags, early indications of a softening in
residential construction activity have become visible in
recent months following the declines in established
housing prices. HIA new home sales, a leading indicator

of dwelling demand, have declined materially, and many
builders in the liaison program have reported a decline
in enquiries, buyer conversion rates and sales.

Reserve Bank of Australia  |  Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 44

### Page 48

Public demand growth has
moderated over the past year.
Public demand was weaker than expected over the
first half of the year, with growth moderating from
elevated rates observed over recent years. An easing
in public consumption growth reflected the ending of
some temporary cost-of-living measures, alongside
moderation in some categories of government spending
such as defence and disability and aged care services.
Public investment has moved around a broadly stable

level since late 2024, and budget projections continue to
suggest that there is a substantial pipeline of public
infrastructure projects.
Exports supported GDP growth in
the June quarter, though the
strength largely reflected temporary
supply factors and growth has
slowed since late 2025.
Exports increased by 0.8 per cent in the quarter, in
line with expectations and driven by resource and
manufactured exports, though year-ended growth
slowed further. A rebound in resource exports largely

reflected a recovery from weather-related disruptions in
the previous quarter, met by strong mining inventory
drawdowns. As expected, the effect of the Middle East
conflict on resource exports has largely operated
through higher commodity prices and export earnings;
production and export volumes are unlikely to respond
materially to price movements unless higher prices are
expected to persist, given the long lead times for new
investments. Services exports have declined this year,

reflecting declines in both education and tourism
exports, with support from stronger global growth offset
by changes to student visa settings, some disruption to
international travel from the Middle East conflict, and the
appreciation of the Australian dollar since late last year.
The boom in AI-related activity globally does not appear
to have had a material influence on Australia’s exports, as
production of critical minerals remains small and the

expansion in data centre capacity has been to service
domestic demand for computing capacity.

Reserve Bank of Australia  |  Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 45

### Page 49

Labour market conditions have eased by more than
expected recently. This easing is judged largely to reflect
stronger-than-expected labour supply, rather than an
unexpected weakening in labour demand. Elevated
cost-of-living pressures may be leading some people to
seek additional hours or enter the labour force, which
may be contributing to the increase in labour supply.
Timely indicators of labour demand, such as job
advertisements, are generally consistent with stable

growth in labour demand in the near term, though
signals differ across the measures. Despite the recent
easing in conditions, we continue to assess that the
labour market remains a little tight (see section
2.5 Assessment of spare capacity). Wages growth was
stable over the first half of 2026 and in line with
expectations, though growth in unit labour costs picked
up in the first half of the year to be further above
average, and a little stronger than anticipated. Liaison

contacts’ expectations for wages growth over the year
ahead remain elevated, driven by an expected
continuation of cost-of-living pressures.
Labour market conditions have
eased by more than expected in
recent months.
The unemployment rate was 4.7 per cent in the
month of September, and averaged 4.6 per cent in
the September quarter (Graph 2.16).2 This was higher
than our August Statement forecast of 4.4 per cent for
the quarter and was accounted for by

stronger-than-expected labour force participation rather
than weaker-than-expected employment. The
underemployment rate increased to 6.3 per cent in
September, though remained low by
historical standards.
Over the year to the June quarter, employment
growth was increasingly driven by the market
sector, with construction making the largest
contribution to growth. In the September quarter, the
employment-to-population ratio was 63.8 per cent, in
line with expectations in August (Graph 2.17). After

declining for a few months, average hours per worker
have partially recovered recently, to be near their levels
at the beginning of the year (Graph 2.17).

The participation rate increased to 66.9 per cent in

pressures – such as elevated cost-of-living pressures –
which may be encouraging people to enter or remain in
the labour force. The share of employed persons holding

6.9 per cent in the June quarter.

Leading indicators generally point to stable growth
in labour demand in the near term, although the
signal differs somewhat across measures. Job
advertisements have increased slightly or remained
broadly stable over recent months, while vacancies (as a
share of the labour force) declined in the past quarter
(Graph 2.18). Employment intentions from business
surveys partially rebounded in the quarter and the
measure from the RBA’s liaison program has also

strengthened a little since earlier in the year. Households’
unemployment expectations have ticked back up in
August and September to be a little above their
long-run average.

Aggregate wages growth remained
stable (at a relatively elevated rate) in
the June quarter, as expected, but

liaison contacts report a noticeable
increase in wages growth since July.
The June quarter Wage Price Index (WPI) affirmed
our assessment in the August Statement that growth
in base wages had remained broadly stable. The WPI
increased by 3.2 per cent over the year; private sector
WPI growth remained broadly stable at 0.7 per cent in
the quarter and eased to 3.1 per cent over the year, as
expected (Graph 2.19). Wages growth for workers on
individual arrangements – which tends to be most

responsive to current labour market conditions –
remained at 2.9 per cent over the year but eased a little
relative to its pace in 2025, consistent with an easing in
labour market conditions since then. Public sector WPI
growth remained stronger than private sector WPI
growth. Overall, the June quarter data are consistent
with our assessment that labour market conditions
remain a little tight.

Wages growth is expected to have picked up in the
September quarter, in part reflecting the recent Fair
Work Commission (FWC) decision to increase all modern
award wages by 4.75 per cent. Meanwhile, some
pass-through from higher inflation to private enterprise
bargaining agreements and individual arrangements is
expected to have supported wages growth, as workers
seek to preserve recent real wage gains. Consistent with
this, liaison contacts have reported that wages growth

has increased noticeably since July, including for firms
that are not reliant on awards for setting wages. Liaison
contacts’ expectations for wages growth over the year
ahead remain elevated, driven by an expected
continuation of cost-of-living pressures.
Growth in the national accounts’ measure of average
earnings per hour (AENA) eased to 3.2 per cent over
the year to the June quarter. AENA growth was
stronger than forecast in the August Statement,

suggesting there was a little more momentum in labour
costs than previously expected. AENA is a broader
measure of labour costs than the WPI but tends to be
more volatile, and so provides a noisier read on wage
pressures arising from conditions in the labour market. In
real terms, AENA has grown by more than real WPI in
recent years and is above its pre-pandemic level. Despite
the easing in average earnings growth, it remained
elevated relative to the current pace of productivity

growth, and weaker productivity growth contributed to
an increase in growth in unit labour costs (ULCs) in the
first half of this year (Graph 2.20). At 3.5 per cent in
year-ended terms, ULCs growth remained significantly
above the rate that is consistent with inflation being at
target, contributing to ongoing domestic cost pressures
and pointing to some tightness in labour
market conditions.

### Page 52

Despite the recent slowing in demand growth and
easing in labour market conditions, we continue to
assess that there are some capacity pressures in the
Australian economy. Further, those capacity pressures
are now assessed to have been a little greater over the
first half of the year than we thought at the time of the
August Statement. A number of factors support this
judgement, including: strong ongoing domestically
generated cost and price pressures, particularly in

components most sensitive to domestic capacity
pressures; continued weak productivity outcomes; and
survey evidence pointing to elevated capacity pressures.
Consistent with that, we have raised our assessment of
the non-accelerating inflation rate of unemployment
(NAIRU) a little. A higher NAIRU implies tighter labour
market conditions for a given unemployment rate. As a
result, despite actual unemployment having increased
recently, we assess that the labour market remains a little

tight, broadly as anticipated in August.
Labour market conditions have
eased over recent months, but are
judged to remain a little tight.
Most of the labour market indicators we monitor
suggest some easing in conditions over recent
months. But a majority are close to or slightly tighter
than their estimated trend levels (Graph 2.21).3 The
underemployment rate, hours-based underutilisation
rate and quits rate in particular point to some tightness
in the labour market.

2.5 Assessment of spare capacity

Model-based estimates of the NAIRU have gradually
moved higher over recent years. The model average is
now around 5 per cent (Graph 2.22), although these
estimates are just one input to our overall assessment of
the NAIRU (see below), alongside evidence from the
broader suite of indicators and judgement. Model-based
estimates have moved higher in response to persistently
elevated unit labour cost growth and inflation over
recent years.

We now assess the NAIRU to be slightly higher than
was factored into the August forecasts. For some time
we have noted that our model-based estimates have
been pointing to a higher NAIRU – and therefore more
capacity pressures – than the broader suite of labour
market indicators (Graph 2.23). Our overall assessment

has generally taken more signal from the broader set of
indicators. However, underlying inflation and labour cost
growth have been persistently elevated, and stronger
than expected recently (see section 2.6 Inflation). These
outcomes suggest putting a little more weight on the
model-based estimates than we had been doing.
Accordingly, we are increasing our assessment of the
NAIRU to around 43⁄4 per cent. This is a modest
adjustment when compared with the range of

uncertainty around estimates from either the models or
broader set of indicators.

Given this judgement, we continue to assess that
the labour market is a little tight, despite the recent
easing in a number of indicators. The revision to our
NAIRU estimate implies that the labour market was a
little tighter through the first half of this year than
previously assessed. It also implies that there was a small
amount of tightness remaining in the September

quarter, despite the unemployment rate having
increased by more than expected in recent months
(because the unemployment rate remained a little
below our upwardly revised assessment of the NAIRU).
That is consistent with the signal from the broader suite
of labour market indicators for the September quarter,
which continue to suggest a little tightness in labour
market conditions.
Chapter 2  |  Economic ConditionsEconomic ConditionsEconomic ConditionsEconomic ConditionsEconomic ConditionsEconomic ConditionsEconomic Conditions

Reserve Bank of Australia  |  Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 50

### Page 54

Economy-wide indicators continue
to suggest that some capacity
pressures remain in the broader
economy, consistent with the signal
from the labour market. The June
quarter output gap is now estimated
to have been a little larger than
assessed in August.
Slower productivity growth has lowered the
economy’s potential growth rate compared with
previous decades. The level of productivity is broadly
unchanged since the end of 2019. This has contributed

to capacity pressures persisting despite relatively modest
GDP growth in recent years. Combined with resilient
wages growth and elevated growth in non-labour input
costs, the lack of productivity improvement is likely to
have limited firms’ ability to absorb higher costs without
raising output prices and to have contributed to inflation
being above the target range.
Indicators of capacity utilisation continue to point to
there being some capacity pressures in the

economy. Signals across sectors are mixed, but the NAB
measure of capacity utilisation remains above its
historical average. There is also marked variation across
states, with business liaison suggesting that parts of
Queensland in particular continue to face capacity
constraints, whereas conditions are less tight in Victoria.
Taking survey evidence together with model-based
estimates of the output gap, we assess that the
output gap was positive in the June and September

quarters (Graph 2.24) and slightly larger than
assessed in August. That reflects both
stronger-than-expected real activity data and a small
downward judgement applied to the level of potential
output. This is consistent with our judgement on the
NAIRU; other things equal, a higher NAIRU implies lower
potential supply capacity. Our judgement on potential
output and the output gap is relatively modest
compared with the uncertainty around estimates of

capacity pressures; the range of model estimates and
responses to the RBA survey of market economists in
August suggests the output gap could be between zero
and 11⁄2 per cent. An alternative rationale for
downwardly adjusting the level of potential output
would be to take greater signal from the weakness in
actual productivity outcomes for the trend level of
productivity. We have not changed our expectation of
potential output growth and productivity growth in the

medium term.

Inflation in the September quarter was stronger than
anticipated in the August forecasts and remained well
above target, as some of the upside risks identified in
August materialised. Retail fuel prices made the largest
contribution to the upside surprise in headline inflation.
We judge that domestic capacity pressures also made a
stronger-than-expected contribution to both underlying
and headline inflation in the quarter. Wider cost
pressures related to the Middle East conflict have

contributed to inflation, with the size of these effects
having been broadly as anticipated so far (excluding the
direct effect of retail fuel prices). Measures of cost
growth remain elevated, with renewed pressure on fuel
costs following the re-escalation of the conflict and
ongoing cost pressures from wages, technology and
services. Some measures of inflation expectations –
including longer-term measures – have increased, and
this may be contributing to inflation if it is influencing

households’ and firms’ expectations for wages and cost
growth. Households and firms have increasingly been
paying greater attention to inflation, which may make
their inflation expectations more sensitive to
future shocks.
Headline inflation in the September
quarter was stronger than
anticipated in the August Statement.
In year-ended terms, headline inflation was
unchanged at 3.9 per cent in the September quarter.
This outcome reflected stronger-than-expected

underlying inflation, as well as stronger-than-expected
growth in automotive fuel prices because of a material
increase in regional benchmark prices for refined fuel
(see section 2.1 Commodity price developments)
(Graph 2.3). Fuel contributed around 0.4 percentage
points to year-ended headline inflation in the quarter,
which was higher than expected at the time of the
August Statement. Headline inflation in the month of
September increased to 4.3 per cent, consistent with

ongoing upward pressure from fuel prices (Graph 2.25).

Underlying inflation picked up in the
September quarter by more than

Trimmed mean inflation increased to 1.0 per cent in
the September quarter, from 0.8 per cent in the June
quarter, and picked up to 3.7 per cent in year-ended

and remained elevated in year-ended terms

(Graph 2.26). A majority of items in the CPI (by count and

the inflation target, and this share has increased since
the beginning of the year. We assess that the upward
surprise in underlying inflation reflects
stronger-than-expected domestic capacity pressures this
year. Inflation for market services (excluding
telecommunication and domestic travel), which is
typically among the most sensitive to domestic

conditions, increased and was materially stronger than
expected in the August forecasts; some of this strength
reflects the award wage increase.

Cost pressures related to the Middle
East conflict are judged to have
contributed to higher prices in the
September quarter.
Conflict-related cost pressures are estimated to have
contributed around 0.1 percentage points to
quarterly trimmed mean inflation (excluding the
direct effect of higher retail fuel prices), broadly as
expected in the August Statement. However, these
cost pressures appear to have affected a broader range
of prices in the quarter than previously anticipated.

Increases in building materials costs are judged to have
had less effect on new dwellings inflation than expected.
However, information from RBA liaison suggests
conflict-related cost pressures contributed to price
increases for other items, including some market
services and durable goods. Many liaison contacts have
continued to report higher fuel costs, while some have
also continued to note ongoing concerns about higher
costs for oil-derived products such as plastics following

the re-escalation of tensions in the Middle East (see
Box C: Insights from Liaison).
Broader measures of cost growth from business
surveys and RBA liaison remain elevated (Graph 2.27).
Liaison contacts report that a broad range of cost
pressures – including for wages, technology and services
– are contributing to expectations of continued elevated
cost growth over the year ahead. AI-related cost
pressures have had only a modest impact on consumer

prices to date, but strong price growth for
technology-related goods and services such as
computers and software is contributing to firms’ cost
pressures. Firms are also noting potential future risks to
food supply chains arising from El Niño and avian
influenza, which could put further upward pressure on
costs. Firms’ expectations for aggregate inflation are also
elevated, which may be reinforcing expectations of
strong growth in their own costs.

Survey and liaison data suggest that many firms
have not yet been able to fully pass through
increases in labour and non-labour costs to
consumer prices, resulting in downward pressure on

margins over the past few months. However, in the
Business Outlook Scenario Survey (BOSS) in September,
firms reported that they plan to continue to increase
prices in response to past cost increases arising from the
Middle East conflict, and that they plan to pass through
a greater share of these future cost increases to prices
than they had planned in April.4 Moreover, firms that
experienced larger changes in costs in response to the
conflict have increased the frequency with which they

adjust prices. Consistent with this, liaison contacts are
not expecting growth in costs, wages and selling prices
to ease much over the year ahead.
Inflation was elevated across most
sectors in the September quarter
and picked up for market services,
rents and consumer durables.
Market services inflation (excluding
telecommunications and domestic travel) increased
to 1.3 per cent in the September quarter, from
1.1 per cent in the June quarter. In year-ended terms,

it increased to 4.2 per cent (Graph 2.28). We assess that
this strength primarily reflected domestic capacity
pressures and, to a lesser extent, pass-through of
conflict-related cost pressures (such as from food and
fuel inputs). Some of the recent increase in inflation, for
example in the prices of restaurant meals, likely reflected
the impact of the FWC award wage decision (which
came into effect on 1 July). While the effect of the award
wage increase on inflation may be short-lived, firms’

apparent ability to pass through this cost increase to
prices is consistent with there being some remaining
capacity pressures.

New dwelling construction prices increased by
1.0 per cent in the September quarter to be 5.2 per
cent higher over the year (Graph 2.29). This was weaker
than expected in the August Statement, which we judge
reflected smaller conflict-related cost effects than
anticipated. Liaison information suggests that while
homebuilders continue to experience elevated labour
and materials costs, they have been less able to pass
these costs through to prices recently in states where

housing demand has weakened. New dwelling inflation
tends to lag conditions in the established housing
market, so this may be early evidence that the easing in
housing market conditions that began late last year is
beginning to weigh on new dwelling construction
prices (see section 2.3 Domestic economic activity).

Housing Inflation
CPI rent inflation increased to 0.9 per cent in the
September quarter, from 0.6 per cent in the June
quarter, and was 3.6 per cent in year-ended terms.
The pick-up in the quarter was stronger than expected
in the August Statement. This may be early evidence that
the earlier strong growth in advertised rents – arising
from an imbalance between housing supply and

demand, as reflected in low vacancy rates – is beginning
to pass through to CPI rents, following limited
pass-through over the year to the June quarter. More
recently, vacancy rates have increased modestly and
growth in advertised rents has slowed.
Retail goods inflation was stronger than expected in
the September quarter, largely driven by a pick-up
in consumer durables inflation. This outcome
reflected higher prices for some consumer electronics

(such as computers, televisions and gaming consoles)
driven by strong memory chip cost growth associated
with demand for AI-enabling inputs (Graph 2.30).
Although it is too early to judge the persistence of these
price rises, similar price increases from AI-related cost
pressures have been observed in other advanced
economies (see section 2.2 Global economic conditions).

Inflation Expectations
Longer-term inflation expectations are mostly

consistent with the inflation target, although some
measures have increased. Financial market measures
of inflation expectations remain consistent with inflation
at target over the longer term. However, other
longer-term inflation expectations have increased. The
median market economist now expects inflation to
average 2.6 per cent over the next 5–10 years. Firms’
longer-term inflation expectations have also increased
since mid-2025, and the BOSS survey suggests firms

have become less confident that inflation will return to
target, a sentiment also shared by some contacts in the
liaison program.
Elevated inflation expectations may be contributing
to higher realised inflation if they are influencing
households’ and firms’ expectations for wages and
cost growth. Households and firms are increasingly
paying more attention to inflation, which may make
their inflation expectations more backward-looking and
more sensitive to future shocks (Graph 2.32). The

heightened attentiveness of households and firms to
inflation – together with the re-escalation of the conflict
– raises the likelihood that elevated inflation
expectations feed into price- and wage-setting
decisions, thereby posing a key upside risk to the
outlook for wages growth and inflation (see Chapter 3:
Outlook).

Businesses across most industries continue to

This Box highlights key messages collected by the RBA’s liaison teams based in New South Wales, Queensland,
South Australia, Victoria and Western Australia during discussions with around 240 businesses, industry bodies,
government agencies and community organisations from early-August to end-October 2026.
Liaison contacts report that cost and price pressures remain elevated and are expected to ease only

moderately over the year ahead (Graph C.1). Wages growth outcomes and expectations have picked up since
the middle of the year. Demand conditions generally remain around longer-run averages, with softening
conditions in retail and dwelling investment being broadly offset by relatively stronger conditions in other
areas of business activity. Expectations for growth in demand, investment and employment are also close to
their longer-run averages, although with some variation across regions.

Cost and price pressures remain broadly based and elevated and are
expected to ease only a little over the year ahead.
Businesses continue to report widespread increases in operating costs. Higher fuel costs are flowing
through in the form of surcharges and general transport-related expenses. While some firms are singling
this out as a specific cost driver, others report that fuel-related costs are increasingly mingled into the broader
elevated operating cost environment they are facing. Higher global costs for other oil-derived products are also

flowing through to offshore and domestic supply chains and to consumer prices, though some contacts note
that the appreciation of the Australian dollar is helping to moderate some of this impact.
There is also ongoing upward pressure on other cost base components. Faster growth in IT costs, in part
driven by global competition for memory chips, has been highlighted by many firms. Where firms are
purchasing services from global and domestic IT vendors, these firms are passing through their own upstream

costs into service and subscription prices. Other frequently cited components of operating cost increases
include occupancy expenses, professional services, other labour-intensive third-party services, government

charges and regulatory compliance costs. Many contacts observe that these categories have experienced

repeated increases over recent years. Firms are generally expecting cost growth to remain above longer-run
averages over the year ahead.
Reflecting this cost environment, price increases – both business-to-business and consumer-facing –
remain broadly based across firms in the liaison program (Graph C.2). Over three-quarters of firms reported
increasing prices over the past year; this share had drifted a little lower through to 2025 but picked up again

over the past year. In contrast to the experience pre-pandemic, very few firms reported that prices had been
reduced or held steady over the past year.
Within this, the size of increases in consumer-facing
prices has been mixed, in part reflecting different
demand conditions. Price increases for household
goods have been relatively modest in general,
although an exception has been consumer
electronics given global IT hardware cost
developments. New detached dwelling price

growth is increasingly uneven across states. Builders
have generally been able to pass cost increases
through to prices in markets where demand
conditions are stronger and land supply relatively
tight, whereas pass-through has been more limited
in Victoria and New South Wales, with some
contacts in those states reporting some
discounting to support sales.
Looking ahead, the share of firms expecting to
increase prices is elevated and has increased

further over recent months. Firms generally
expect above-average increases in selling prices in the year ahead. Given firms generally see little prospect of
moderating upstream cost pressures, in many cases contacts note that margins are under pressure and
therefore they continue to look for ways to adjust their cost structures to manage this.
Household spending growth has been fairly steady overall, although
discretionary spending is softer than earlier in the year.

Food retailers continue to report solid growth in consumption spending, broadly tracking trends in
population and household income growth. In contrast, household goods retailers report that
conditions are more challenging now than they were six months or so ago. Contacts attribute this to
persistent household budget pressures, including from higher interest rates and the effects of the Middle East
conflict on fuel prices. Consistent with this, some contacts have flagged an increase in consumers trading

down and a greater focus on bargain hunting than was seen earlier in the year. Businesses in the tourism sector
similarly report softer conditions than earlier in the year, although major sporting and cultural events continue
to generate strong demand. Contacts generally expect that current softer conditions for consumer
discretionary spending will continue into 2027.
Retailers with exposure to multiple states report better conditions in Queensland and Western Australia and

relatively weaker conditions in Victoria. Higher fuel prices and general cost-of-living pressures are reported to
be having a greater effect in remote areas and for lower-income households than in major urban centres and
higher-income groups.

### Page 63

As has been the case for some time, community services organisations report elevated demand for
their services, with a growing trend of more acute and complex needs. Over recent years there has been a
larger cohort of people looking for support from these organisations to address housing stress and budget
pressures, including dual-income households.
Housing construction activity is currently solid, but declining forward
sales point to a weaker outlook …

Residential builders in most markets report solid activity levels at present, with earlier strength in sales
supporting current work pipelines. This is expected to maintain momentum in construction activity into
early 2027; contacts in Sydney and Melbourne report around six months of work compared with longer
pipelines for those operating elsewhere. Softer activity is expected over the year ahead, given the decline in
sales since earlier in 2026. Builders and developers variously attribute this to higher interest rates, tax reforms,

and broader economic uncertainty. Several contacts have reported more buyers are adopting a ‘wait-and-see’
approach, reflected in longer decision-making timeframes and weaker sales, as well as some increase in
contract cancellation rates over recent months.
Apartment builders continue to report affordability constraints and poor project feasibility as key
factors weighing on development activity, with viable projects mainly concentrated in more premium

segments of the market. Contacts note that demand fundamentals – strong population growth, low
unemployment and ongoing housing shortages in many markets – are supportive, but there remains a
significant gap between current market pricing and the price at which most apartments can be profitably built.
… while conditions for non-residential construction and other business
investment are more resilient.
Firms’ investment intentions have been resilient over recent months and remain a little above longer-run

averages. Within this, a number of businesses have noted upward revisions to capex costs over recent months,
in part due to increases in construction and technology costs, but this has not materially changed plans. Firms
with above-average investment intentions are primarily spending on projects to expand their businesses and
to slow growth in operating costs. Commonly cited project types include construction to expand sites and
capacity, new machinery and equipment (including automation), and technology programs.

Consistent with this, firms in the non-residential construction sector report solid demand conditions;
public and private sector spending is fairly strong at present, and contacts are anticipating a good pipeline of
activity over the next year or so across many regions. Firms associated with data centre developments continue
to report particularly buoyant conditions, although contacts have flagged some uncertainty from approval
processes and constraints on construction resource availability, as well as fluidity in underlying client demand.

Developers of renewable energy generation assets generally see the data centre pipeline as a positive for their
sector, but note that these renewables projects are more difficult to progress than was the case a few years ago
and also typically involve longer timelines than data centres. Challenges cited include approval delays, longer
investment decision-making timetables and interdependencies with grid expansions. Revenue projections for
some proposals could also be at risk if the relatively low and stable wholesale electricity prices seen over the

past year were sustained.
Some contacts in the higher education sector note discretionary capex is being deferred or scaled back given
reduced fee revenue from weaker international student enrolments; instead, capex is being increasingly
concentrated in committed projects and essential maintenance. Current and expected spending across other
categories of public investment remain solid, including in defence, transport infrastructure, water utilities and

healthcare projects in many regions.

Firms report hiring intentions are around their long-run average and

wages growth has strengthened.
Consistent with broadly steady demand conditions, firms’ employment intentions are currently sitting
close to their long-run average, having strengthened a little since earlier in the year (when some contacts
noted uncertainty in hiring plans during the acute period of the Middle East conflict and oil price increases).
Contacts expecting headcount growth report this as being supported by project work, expansion activity,

ongoing growth in regulatory and compliance obligations, as well as underlying demand conditions. Firms’
characterisations of labour market conditions have not changed materially over recent months. While
recruiting and retaining staff is generally reported to be easier than a couple of years ago, some firms note
competition for labour remains strong, particularly in sectors such as health care, mining, transport and
construction trades, though with some variation across regions.

Labour costs remain a focus for firms, amid a recent pick-up in wages growth. Reported wages growth
has lifted to be around 33⁄4 per cent over the September quarter, a marked move upwards from earlier in the
year (Graph C.3). This is consistent with the flow-through since July of the Fair Work Commission’s 4.75 per cent
award wage increase decision. Firms also note general cost-of-living pressures and labour market tightness in
some areas as factors influencing wage outcomes over recent months. Contacts expect these factors to also

bear on outcomes over the year ahead and are generally not expecting wages growth to slow materially from
the current pace.
Given this, many contacts have noted they are
looking to drive efficiency gains from improved
rostering, staff reallocations and technological
improvements. For most firms, AI has not affected
headcount and is not expected to do so over the
next year. Many firms are hopeful that AI-driven
productivity gains will support more efficient use of

existing staff resources and may influence future
hiring decisions. To date, though, firms have
reported mixed productivity experiences; while
some more routine tasks are being successfully
automated, some contacts have noted the
increased overhead associated with exploring and
implementing AI as well as responding to
AI-generated material from customers and other
third parties.

### Page 65

Summary
Chapter 3
Outlook
• The outlook for inflation has been revised materially higher, especially in the earlier
part of the forecast period; the forecasts assume that the cash rate follows the
market path. A number of upside risks flagged in the August Statement have since been
incorporated into the central inflation forecast. Fuel and other commodity prices affected
by the ongoing Middle East conflict are expected to contribute more to underlying and

headline inflation than previously expected. Capacity constraints are also judged to have
been somewhat greater throughout this year than we thought in August and inflation
expectations have increased, adding to inflationary pressures. Underlying inflation is now
expected to remain above 3 per cent until late 2027 and to reach 21⁄2 per cent
around mid-2028.
• Headline inflation is expected to remain above 3 per cent until late 2027, around
three quarters longer than in the August forecast. Headline inflation is expected to

peak at 4.5 per cent in the December 2026 quarter and reach the midpoint of the
2–3 per cent inflation target a bit later than was expected in August. The decline in
inflation is expected to reflect conflict-related cost pressures easing and spare capacity
emerging in the labour market and economy more broadly. Risks to the domestic inflation
outlook are more balanced than they were in the August Statement, but still skewed to the
upside. This reflects the incorporation in the central inflation forecast of some of the risks

identified in August. However, inflation could be higher if global and domestic cost
pressures are more persistent than assumed, or if inflation expectations rise further or exert
greater influence on wage- and price-setting.
• The outlook for growth in Australia’s major trading partners has been revised
higher, owing to a stronger outlook for AI-related activity and, more broadly,
resilient demand. Growth is still expected to moderate from its current strong pace over

the next couple of years, as the boost from AI-related activity slows and growth in China
continues to ease. The risks to the outlook are relatively balanced. AI-related activity could
continue to be stronger than expected, while ongoing higher energy prices could weigh
on growth by more than anticipated. The rise in global interest rates presents risks on both
sides of the outlook: it may signal stronger demand and investment than currently
expected, but may also result in tighter financial conditions than assumed.

• The global inflation outlook has also been revised higher, reflecting renewed
pressure from energy and other commodity prices, resilient demand and rapid
growth in AI-related prices. The oil price assumption underpinning the forecasts is much
higher than in August, reflecting the re-escalation of tensions in the Middle East in recent
months. Consensus forecasters expect headline inflation to remain above central bank
targets into 2027 in many advanced economies as a result of higher energy costs. More

broadly, increases in a range of commodity prices and strong AI-related demand are
driving up input costs, which are expected to be passed through to inflation to some
degree. The risks to global inflation are judged to remain skewed to the upside.
Reserve Bank of Australia  |  Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 62

### Page 66

• Australian GDP growth is expected to remain below estimates of potential growth
over coming years. Based on financial market pricing, the forecasts assume the cash rate
will increase by around 35 basis points by mid-2027; this cash rate path is up to 50 basis
points higher than was assumed in the August forecasts. Compared with August, the
outlook for GDP growth over the year ahead has been revised higher, driven by a stronger
outlook for data centre investment. From mid-2027, however, forecast growth has been

revised lower because of the higher assumed cash rate path and a weaker outlook for the
housing market. Higher-than-expected global energy prices is also expected to weigh a
little on activity. Overall, the forecast level of GDP at end-2028 is broadly unchanged from
the August forecasts. The risks to domestic growth appear to be broadly balanced. There
are upside risks to global and domestic economic activity from AI-related demand, but
economic activity could be weaker if the Middle East conflict and the decline in housing

prices affect the economy by more than assumed in the central forecast.
• Labour market conditions are expected to ease further, consistent with
below-potential GDP growth. The unemployment rate is forecast to increase to
5.1 per cent by end-2028. This is around 1⁄4 percentage points higher than previously
expected, largely reflecting the higher starting point for the unemployment rate and an
expectation that more people will remain in or join the labour force.

• Despite this, we assess that capacity constraints in the economy and labour market
have been a little tighter than previously judged, contributing more to wage and
inflationary pressures (see Chapter 2: Economic Conditions). From this starting point, we
judge that aggregate demand and potential supply in the economy are likely to reach
balance next year (slightly later than previously thought), before spare capacity emerges. It
is possible we have misjudged the supply capacity of the economy and labour market.

### Page 67

The central forecast incorporates many judgements,
such as the choice of models used and whether to
deviate from the models given the signal from recent
data or information from liaison. These judgements are
extensively considered and debated during the
construction of the forecast. The three most important
judgements for our current assessment of the economic
outlook are discussed below.
The annual review of the staff’s economic forecasts will
be discussed at the December Board meeting (and the

results will be published following the meeting). The
review will assess what we have learned about the
economy, how the judgements that underpin the
forecasts have fared and how the risks around the
outlook have evolved.
Key judgement #1 – Global oil and
refined product prices decline
gradually but remain above
pre-conflict levels.
The assumption that global oil and related prices decline
gradually but remain above levels prior to the Middle
East conflict underpins the central forecasts (Graph 3.1).

The assumed oil price in the December quarter is
35 per cent higher than assumed in August, following a
re-escalation of the conflict in recent months. From this
higher starting point, oil prices are assumed to be
11 per cent higher by end-2028 than assumed in August.
This assumed decline is consistent with energy markets
continuing to adjust to the conflict and/or progress
towards resolution of the conflict, and is within the
range of the latest Consensus forecasts. The spreads

between prices for refined fuels and Brent crude oil,
which have widened since the start of the conflict, are
assumed to return to their historical averages by
mid-2027 – around two quarters later than assumed in
the August Statement. Oil and refined product prices
could rise further or remain elevated for longer than
assumed, which would drive up global and domestic
inflation and could also weigh on activity (see Key risk #1
and Key risk #3).
